| [How Do Coding-Agent Harnesses Self-Evolve? 2610.34938](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2610.34938) | Oct 2026 | Claude Code `/retro` on 30 SWE-bench issues produced 125 proposals: executable checks 38, navigation pointers 24, instructions 21. 18 of 19 "prior-driven" proposals are lint boilerplate. Only 2 of 7 informative transfers eliminated the target action; incomplete known-failure files added work on unrelated tasks. | Behavioural evidence: agents propose object-level, local, often generic edits and never revise the retro procedure. Adaptations fail when their scope does not match later instances. |
| [BenchPulse hpoWWEDJW9](https://openreview.net/forum?id=hpoWWEDJW9) | Nov 2026 | Census of 4,119 commits across 15 agent benchmarks. A single τ-bench verifier commit moves scores −5.66 pp; τ²-bench releases move panels up to 9 pts; BFCL aggregation changes create 893 strict order reversals. | Real, already-mined drift in verifiers and environments: a ready source in which the feedback channel itself changes, which is exactly what should force improver-level change. |
| [ContractPatch K2fFQX3zz6](https://openreview.net/forum?id=K2fFQX3zz6) | Nov 2026 | 105 real-schema REST/MCP migration cases, including an untouched Notion release pair. LLMs make "target-valid unsafe" migrations; a verifier abstains when no unique correspondence exists. | Proves real API release pairs can be harvested and certified. A source and a safety metric for real API drift. |
| [LibEvoBench 2606.25402](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2606.25402), [GitChameleon 2507.12367](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2507.12367), [RustEvo² 2503.16922](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2503.16922), [CODESYNC 2502.16645](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2502.16645), [CodeUpdateArena 2407.06249](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2407.06249), [Bridge 2608.30497](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2608.30497), [Deprecated Python APIs 2609.32618](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2609.32618) | 2024–Sep 2026 | Real library and API version evolution (RustEvo² mines 588 real API changes; CodeUpdateArena synthesizes updates†). Models are "version-oblivious". Bridge mines ecosystem-scale API update mappings with client update instances. | The richest real-drift data, but all one-shot model evaluation or editing, not agent continual adaptation. Bridge plus a deprecation corpus is a ready stream generator. |
| [SWE-bench-Live 2505.23419](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2505.23419), [Claw-Eval-Live 2604.28139](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2604.28139), [SWE-Time 2611.12083](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2611.12083), [TestEvo-Bench 2607.02469](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2607.02469) | 2025–Nov 2026 | Live or time-stamped refreshable benchmarks from real GitHub issues and workflow-demand signals. | Contamination-proof by time split, but each release is evaluated independently; no carried state. |
| [FutureSim 2605.15188](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2605.15188), [ReLiveGym 2610.00710](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2610.00710), [RAG or Learning? 2604.05096](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2604.05096), [OAKS 2603.07392](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2603.07392) | Mar–Sep 2026 | Chronological replay of real news, markets and social media; real time-stamped knowledge drift. Best FutureSim agent accuracy is 25%. | Shows the replay-reality paradigm works and stays beyond the cutoff. Knowledge or forecasting only, with no procedural or meta drift. |
| [Seven-Year Itch 2e5z57jIPK](https://openreview.net/forum?id=2e5z57jIPK) | Nov 2026 | Drifting-user model: past some history length, more active memory reduces adaptation. Eight forms of user change. | A theory-backed crossover: stale experience has a cost. Supports making "how much authority the past keeps" a meta-parameter the improver must own. |
| [Do NNs Lose Plasticity in a Gradually Changing World? hip2PfZSRg](https://openreview.net/forum?id=hip2PfZSRg); [Can Scale Overcome Plasticity Loss? 0H9Qwt6mOj](https://openreview.net/forum?id=0H9Qwt6mOj); Dohare et al. 2024 *Nature* ("Loss of plasticity in deep continual learning", title verified on S2) | 2024–Nov 2026 | Loss of plasticity scales with the abruptness of transitions and is much reduced under gradual change. Its onset follows a sublinear scaling law in model size. | Real drift is mostly gradual, so synthetic abrupt block switches may overstate forgetting and plasticity loss. A real-vs-synthetic comparison is itself a result. |
| [Measure Learning at Steady State 2609.31640](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2609.31640), [Implicit Assumptions About Tasks in CL 2610.41792](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2610.41792) | Sep–Oct 2026 | Over a 174-question horizon, unbounded ICL's cost doubles at a modest hit gain, inverting CL-Bench's ranking. How a stream is partitioned changes the measured CL quantities. | Horizon and partition choices flip conclusions. A benchmark must pre-register horizons and segment definitions. |
| [Third Eye 2611.12988](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2611.12988), [Identity under Model Transition 2611.10628](https://www.alphaxiv.org/abs/2611.10628), [TAME IXnpSW8g3v](https://openreview.net/forum?id=IXnpSW8g3v) | Nov 2026 | Does a useful self-update stay useful after the next update? Persona, memory and rules reloaded onto an upgraded backend do not preserve behaviour. Harness rank reversal under support change. | Base-model releases are themselves real, time-ordered drift that invalidates evolved harnesses. Not yet used as a benchmark axis. |
| [CUAEvolve / OSEvolveBench 7tsy5BAqPe](https://openreview.net/forum?id=7tsy5BAqPe) | Nov 2026 | Application updates and workflow revisions erode CUA capabilities; a self-improving RL curriculum is synthesized from failure diagnoses. Variants modify one interface property each. | App-update framing, but the variants are synthetic single-property edits. |
| Continual World†, Lyle et al. 2023 "Understanding plasticity"† | 2021–23 | Classic non-stationary RL benchmark and plasticity-loss analysis. | Backward anchors only (not fetched this session). |

### What the evidence says
- **Agentic CL benchmarks almost never use real drift** (strong; about 20 benchmarks checked). Drift comes from re-blocking existing datasets (2610.25858, AgentStream), LLM-generated transforms (Δτ-Bench), injected schema changes (AgentDriftBench, DynaToolBench) or hidden rule mods (MirrorCraft, AdaExploreBench, PACE). CL-Bench is closest to "real tasks", but its own paper calls its drift designed or epistemic.
- **Real drift data is plentiful and already mined, but used only statically** (strong).
  - Library and API histories: LibEvoBench, RustEvo², GitChameleon, CODESYNC, Bridge.
  - Real repo histories with temporal validity: MemDev-Bench.
  - Benchmark and verifier commit histories: BenchPulse.
  - Real REST/MCP schema release pairs: ContractPatch.
  - Replayed news and markets: FutureSim, ReLiveGym.
  - Each is used for one-shot evaluation or memory evaluation, never for a self-improver's lifetime.
- **Object-level adaptation is always the path of least resistance** (strong, convergent). The evidence comes from four directions:
  - SelfImprove-Bench: 0% improver edits unless forced; Improvement by Inheritance agrees.
  - `/retro` study: proposals are lint, pointers and known-failure lists.
  - Are-SE-CL: GEPA and Meta-Harness edit shared prompts; the MH gate freezes the workspace.
  - Δτ: skill methods give a one-time v1 gain.
  
  None of these benchmarks makes the improver's procedure the binding constraint, so not updating the improver is rational behaviour, not a capability failure.
- **Persistence improves speed but not end quality** (moderate-strong). Continual AutoResearch (final gain about 0 for 4 of 5 agents), CL-Bench (dedicated memory loses to ICL), MemDev-Bench (memory harms 5–10% of solvable tasks) and ContinualSkillBench (skills ≈ raw context) all agree. Forward transfer of improvement ability is therefore currently near zero.
- **Global self-modification interferes; localized retrieval collides** (moderate; one model and three methods in 2610.25858). The plasticity–stability trade-off holds across methods with r = −0.72.
- **Drift abruptness matters** (moderate, weight-level only: hip2PfZSRg). Real drift is mostly gradual, so results from abrupt synthetic streams may not transfer to reality. This is untested for harness learners.
- **Benchmarks and verifiers drift too** (strong: BenchPulse). A self-improver that tunes to a verifier faces real feedback-channel drift; τ-bench moved −5.66 pp from one verifier commit.

### Debates and contradictions
- **ICL vs dedicated memory.** CL-Bench says ICL wins. 2609.31640 shows that at steady state ICL's cost doubles at little hit gain, and MemDev-Bench's FullTraj wins on steps. The verdict depends on horizon and cost accounting, which no benchmark pre-registers.
- **Do improvers transfer?** RSI-Harness (EOrtreQLbv) claims improver transfer up to 4.5×. RSIBench reports −8.3 pp held-out, and Continual AutoResearch reports near-zero final transfer. None of them tests transfer across real time slices.
- **Synthetic vs real drift.** Synthetic gives control: certification à la EnvOS and paired worlds à la MirrorCraft and BreakingWeb. Real gives validity but confounds (difficulty changes, solution leakage; MemDev saw 93% vs 73% resolve with web access). Nobody has measured whether method rankings agree between the two. WebVariants' 50% rank reversals across implementations suggest they may not.
- **Should stale experience be forgotten or kept?** The Seven-Year Itch says past a crossover, keep less. Δτ/STEWARD keeps inactive skills for reactivation. Continual-ARC's dormant-return design rewards retention. A real-drift stream with reverting changes (deprecation then un-deprecation, patch rollbacks) would adjudicate.
- **Is "the improver never self-edits" a bug or a benchmark artefact?** The anchors treat it as a capability gap. Here it is better read as an incentive gap: on stationary or object-level-solvable streams, the optimal improver change is no change.

### Open gaps
1. **No benchmark uses real chronological drift for a self-improving agent's lifetime.**
   - *Why open now:* MemDev-Bench's temporally valid PR pipeline, Bridge's ecosystem-scale API update mappings, ContractPatch's certified real schema pairs and BenchPulse's 4,119-commit census all appeared in Aug–Nov 2026. The components exist but nobody has chained them into a stream with carried harness state.
   - *Data:* PyPI/npm release diffs and DeprecationWarnings; OpenAPI and MCP schema diffs†; the GitHub REST API version-header history†; Wayback snapshots of the BreakingWeb/WebHarbor sites†; game patch notes (Pokémon Showdown or LoL rule changes†); yearly tax-rule tables†; Wikipedia revisions; benchmark-repo verifier commits.
2. **No benchmark makes object-level adaptation provably insufficient.**
   - *Proposed design:* change the drift type, not just its content, across eras. For example: rename-only era → silent semantic-change era (same signature, new behaviour) → deprecation-with-shim era → verifier-change era.
   - *Mechanism:* a fixed improver procedure such as "append a rule after failure" or "accept if the validation score rises" becomes systematically wrong in a later era. Sound handling requires changing the procedure itself, e.g. adding a probe-before-reuse step, version-scoping memories, or re-validating old rules on schedule.
   - *Why open now:* SelfImprove-Bench forced arms and Metaⁿ depth stalls show the improver-edit arm is empty under current tasks. Real data now makes era-typed drift constructible.
3. **No metric of forward transfer of improvement ability.**
   - *Proposed metric:* checkpoint the improver at generation g. Start it fresh on a later, unseen time slice with a reset object-level state. Measure held-out gain per $ against a generation-0 improver and against a matched-compute fixed improver. Add a scrubbed-memory control in the Continual AutoResearch matched/shuffled/none style.
   - *Why open now:* Agent Plasticity defines actor plasticity only, and every other benchmark reports object-level P/S/I/F.
4. **Time-split contamination control has not been combined with carried state.**
   - *Situation:* the live benchmarks (SWE-bench-Live, Claw-Eval-Live, FutureSim) refresh releases but evaluate each independently. MemDev-Bench shows web or recall leakage can lift resolve rates by 20 pp.
   - *Proposed design:* use only drift events after the model cutoff (releases from Jun 2026 on for current models), with web isolation.
   - *Why open now:* enough post-cutoff release history has accumulated since mid-2026.
5. **Base-model upgrades are unused as a real drift axis.**
   - *Situation:* Third Eye, Identity-under-Transition, TAME and UpgradeBench (prior context) show evolved state breaks across releases. Model release dates give a natural real stream (e.g. Qwen/Llama point releases).
   - *Why open now:* several same-family open-weight releases appeared in 2026†.
6. **Abrupt vs gradual, and synthetic vs real, ranking agreement is unmeasured for harness learners.**
   - *Proposed test:* run identical methods on a real-history stream and on its synthetic analogue, matched in change counts per era, and test rank agreement.
   - *Why open now:* hip2PfZSRg gives the weight-level prediction, and Δτ/AgentDriftBench give the synthetic analogues.
7. **Feedback-channel drift is unstudied.**
   - *Situation:* real verifier commits (BenchPulse) would force a self-improver to revise its acceptance gate, the component that is otherwise always frozen.
   - *Why open now:* T04ZzT5m6j, TRUST and the gate-drift theory in prior context show gates are the crux, but they are always tested against fixed judges.

### Where this is heading (6-month forecast)
- **Real-history drift benchmarks will appear (≈70%).** Most likely they will extend MemDev-Bench or SWE-bench-Live to API and version streams (MemoraX, JetBrains LibEvo group, Microsoft SWE-Live). Most will be memory- or skill-centric, not meta-level. The headline "first real-drift continual agent benchmark" is likely to be taken by NeurIPS 2027 submission time. Era-typed drift that defeats object-level adaptation, plus improver forward-transfer metrics, should stay open (about 60% unscooped by May 2027).
- **Δτ-style tool-ecosystem benchmarks will add real MCP registry histories (≈50%).** ContractPatch-style certification will become standard.
- **ICLR 2027 reviews will push CL-Bench/AgentCL-style papers toward horizon and cost pre-registration** (in light of 2609.31640 and BenchPulse). Expect "steady-state gain" and "gain per $" to replace short-horizon gain.
- **Improver-forcing designs will appear as forced-edit arms (SelfImprove-Bench style) rather than environment pressure (≈60%).** An environment-induced need to change the improver, grounded in real eras, is the differentiated contribution.
- **Fit for Dan:** the mentor's BreakingWeb gives a direct path. Replay real DOM history of its self-hosted apps via upstream release tags (e.g. Gitea/Mattermost-style apps†), certify each change EnvOS-style, and score a Gödel-Agent-style improver against a fixed improver on improver forward transfer. A pilot within budget looks feasible: about 200 tasks × 4 eras × 3 seeds on a cheap model. The cost estimate is mine, not taken from any paper.

† = not verified this session: details carried over from prior-round context, a data source I named but did not fetch, or a backward anchor whose abstract I did not pull.


## Ideas

### When the Grader Moves: Real Verifier-Commit Drift Is What Makes Self-Improvers Rewrite Their Acceptance Gate (`reality-verifier-drift-gate`) — panel 66.7/100

**Claim.** Replaying real verifier and benchmark-harness commits (BenchPulse-mined) under a frozen task pool raises the rate at which a Gödel-lite agent edits its own acceptance gate or improver from about 0% to at least 15% of retained edits. A gate-editable arm then keeps at least 50% fewer stale accepted edits than a frozen-gate arm, measured on held-out tasks graded by the post-commit verifier.

**If the opposite holds.** If gate-edit rates stay near 0 and stale-edit retention matches the frozen arm even when the feedback channel changes, then no ordinary environmental pressure induces meta-level change. That strongly supports building improver-forcing mechanisms (scheduled revalidation, explicit meta-budgets) into harness designs, and it is publishable as the clean negative that resolves the 'bug or artefact' debate the review flags.

**Law.** Retained stale edits decay as exp(−t/τ) after a verifier commit. The prediction is that τ is at least 2× shorter in the gate-editable arm, and that τ scales with commit score impact |Δ| (larger |Δ| gives a shorter τ only when S5 is editable).

**Design.** Testbed: the shared Gödel-lite loop, surfaces S1–S5.

Data: pick 3 agent benchmarks with real, mined verifier churn that changes scores (τ-bench / τ²-bench verifier commits, a BFCL aggregation or checker change, one SWE-style test-harness fix). Each verifier version is a pinned git checkout.

Stream: the tasks stay fixed while the grader steps through 4 real commit states V0 to V3. The agent sees only the current verifier's feedback.

Held-out: a 30% task split never used for gating, scored under the post-commit verifier and under a human-validated consensus 'intended' grader.

Arms, at a matched token budget per verifier state:
(a) object-level surfaces only, with a frozen gate;
(b) improver-editable but gate frozen;
(c) gate-editable (S5);
(d) frozen gate plus a hand-written 'revalidate every rule on version bump' oracle procedure, as an upper reference;
(e) a shuffled-commit-order control.
Models: Qwen3-32B on vLLM, plus a cheap API model as a check. Five lineages per arm.

Metrics:
- share of retained edits that touch S4/S5;
- stale accepted edits (edits whose benefit flips sign under the new verifier and that are still retained);
- recovery AUC after each commit (AgentDriftBench-style);
- gap between held-out score under the intended grader and under the current verifier (verifier-overfit gap).
Statistics: negative-binomial GLM for edit counts, mixed-effects models for scores, and bootstrap CIs over lineages.

**Pilot.** Use 1 benchmark (τ-bench retail, a subset of 60 tasks), 2 real verifier states around the −5.66 pp commit, arms (a) and (c), 3 lineages, a local Qwen3-32B, and about $30 of API calls.
Go if both hold: at least 10% of accepted V0 edits flip benefit under V1, which shows the commit actually stresses retained edits; and arm (c) makes at least one gate edit in at least 2 of 3 lineages.
Kill if fewer than 5% of edits flip. That would mean real verifier drift is too gentle, and the stream should switch to a larger-impact BFCL aggregation change.

**Closest work (author's view).** BenchPulse hpoWWEDJW9 provides the census of real verifier drift but no learning agent; the delta is using it as a stream for a self-improver. Breaking-the-improver and gate-dial (prior rounds) use fixed judges and static gates. AgentDriftBench 5xFWggnePr provides recovery metrics but injects environment drift and includes no improver. Are-SE-CL 2610.25858 observes that the Meta-Harness gate freezes but never perturbs the gate's feedback source. SelfImprove-Bench 2611.02797 sees 0% improver edits on stationary graders.

**Scoop check: medium-risk.** I found no paper that replays real, mined verifier or benchmark-harness commits as a stream for a self-improver, or that tests whether grader drift is what causes gate or improver self-edits. The behavioral core of the idea is open. The framing nearby is crowded, though: evaluator revision and false progress (tn4eslVXEK), co-evolving evaluators (Red Queen Gödel Machine, Gödel Ecology, 2610.11464), and the visible-versus-target overfit gap (C6EElQ2PRG). Changes to survive review: (1) Lead with the causal behavioral claim: exogenous, real grader maintenance moves the gate/improver edit share from about 0% to at least 15%, and the shuffled-commit-order control and a synthetic-drift control with matched |Δ| should show that real commits differ from injected noise. Put the stale-edit and overfit-gap metrics second. (2) Present the metrics as an extension of tn4eslVXEK's cross-version matrix to harness-level self-improvement with an editable gate, and say explicitly that the evaluator change here is exogenous. Co-evolution papers control their own evaluator, and real benchmarks change underneath you. (3) Add an arm where the agent sees the verifier diff or changelog versus one where it only sees feedback, to separate 'notices drift' from 'can act on drift'. (4) Treat the exp(−t/τ) law as a secondary, pre-registered prediction, following Masked Erosion's frozen-model style, not as the headline. Most real commits are bug fixes, so the |Δ| range may be narrow. Get BenchPulse's commit list early, and pick commits that cover a wide range of |Δ|, including sign flips. (5) Include an arm that edits the gate under a stationary grader, to rule out 'S5 is simply exposed more' as the explanation.

| Paper | Title | Summary | Overlap |
|---|---|---|---|
| OpenReview tn4eslVXEK | When the Evaluator Changes: Measuring False Progress in Self-Improving Language Models (ICLR 2027 submission) | Studies 'false progress' when the evaluator is revised during self-improvement. Uses a cross-version evaluation matrix (each model version scored under each evaluator version), a progress decomposition, and Loom, a replayable dataflow that tracks which supervision fed which update and whether the model state can recover after the evaluator is repaired. Experiments are controlled RL post-training. | High on measurement. It already covers the evaluator-revision framing, the stale-progress idea, and recoverability after an evaluator fix, and it is the most likely reviewer comparison for the stale-accepted-edits and verifier-overfit-gap metrics. It differs in that it does weight-level RL rather than a harness self-improver, uses synthetic or controlled evaluator revisions rather than mined real verifier commits, and has no editable acceptance gate and no measure of how often gate or improver edits happen. |
| arXiv 2606.26294 | The Red Queen Gödel Machine: Co-Evolving Agents and Their Evaluators | Recursive self-improvement under non-stationary utilities, where learned evaluators (a reviewer agent, graders) co-evolve with the coder or prover. Reports gains over a fixed-evaluator baseline on DeepSWE, paper writing and IMO grading. | Medium. It claims the 'non-stationary evaluator in a Gödel-style loop' framing. But the evaluator change is endogenous and designed in. It does not replay real exogenous grader commits, does not measure whether the agent itself edits its gate, and does not measure stale edits. Its forward citations (VeriEvolve 2610.29005, Verifier-Harness Co-Evolution 2610.17609, Evo² 2610.13025, Self-Modifying Lean Proof Agents 2607.17352) are all co-evolution designs, none drift replay. |
| OpenReview N4GJVmrzsF | Gödel Ecology: Productivity-Guided Continuous Co-Evolution of Agents and Evaluators | Continuous co-evolution of self-improving coding agents and a soft mixture of evaluators. Includes delayed credit assignment and adversarial interventions against self-reinforcing evaluator drift. | Medium. It contains 'evaluator drift' and evaluator updates inside Gödel-style RSI, but the drift is generated by the system itself. There is no real verifier-commit stream and no comparison of gate-editable versus frozen-gate arms. |
| OpenReview hpoWWEDJW9 | BenchPulse: Monitoring Verdict and Ranking Drift in Evolving Agent Benchmarks | Census of 4,119 commits across 15 agent benchmarks. A single τ-bench verifier commit moves scores by -5.66 pp, a τ²-bench release moves model panels by up to 9 pp, and a BFCL aggregation change reverses 893 pairwise orderings. Includes a replay panel for re-evaluation. | Data source only. It covers exactly the commits the idea plans to mine, with no learning agent. If BenchPulse publishes its commit list, the data-mining contribution shrinks, so the novelty has to come from the self-improver behavior. |
| arXiv 2610.38392 | Masked Erosion: How Acceptance Gates Lose Redundant Safeguards in Self-Improving Agents | 411 improvement loops under ten acceptance-gate rules, plus GEPA. Shows that the gate fails to see redundant safeguards being deleted, and fits a pre-registered statistical model of where each gate breaks. | Low to medium. It treats the acceptance gate as the object of study and builds a predictive law, but the gate rules are fixed by the experimenter, the grader does not move, and the agent never edits its own gate. |
| OpenReview C6EElQ2PRG | Feedback Alignment in Self-Improving Agents | Separates the visible feedback V used for scaffold selection from a held-out target T. Scaffold evolution raises V while lowering T, and retained scaffolds encode evaluator-specific rules. | Medium on the 'verifier-overfit gap' metric (intended grader versus current verifier). The V/T split is static, with no drift over time and no editing of the gate. |
| OpenReview GX4ucmiKZa / arXiv 2610.27465 / arXiv 2610.11464 | Repeatable but Not Replicable (evaluator state); Recoverability and Verifier Strength; Who Verifies the Verifier? | Respectively: evaluator state as a hidden facet that flips self-evolution decisions; verifier strength σ plus recoverability as preconditions for unattended self-improvement (with τ²-bench experiments); co-evolving inspectable graders anchored to a reference set. | Low. Each covers one neighbouring piece (evaluator instability, gate trust, grader evolution). None uses real verifier version churn as a stream or measures whether drift causes improver or gate edits. |
| OpenReview 5xFWggnePr | AgentDriftBench | Injects mid-evaluation environment drift and reports recovery AUC and recovery steps for Qwen agents. | Low. It supplies the recovery metrics, but the drift is in the environment rather than the grader, and there is no self-improver. |

**Judge panel**

| Lens | N | S | R | C | F | T | Total | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ac | 3 | 3.5 | 4.5 | 2.5 | 3 | 4 | 67.1 | pursue-with-fixes |
| expert | 3 | 3.5 | 4 | 3 | 2.5 | 3.5 | 65 | pursue-with-fixes (K4) |
| mentor | 3.5 | 3 | 4.5 | 3 | 2.5 | 4 | 67.9 | pursue-with-fixes |

- **ac**: strongest: The question is causal and directly answers the user's 'why doesn't the meta-harness update itself' debate. It uses exogenous, real grader maintenance as a natural experiment, which no co-evolution paper does: in those papers the system controls its own evaluator, and SelfImprove-Bench and Are-SE-CL only observe the 0% baseline. Both outcomes are informative, a reusable replay harness made of pinned verifier checkouts can come out of it, and relevance to self-referential meta-agents is high. · weakest: The mechanism and the power are both thin. The agent sees only score feedback, so a verifier commit looks like an unexplained regression, and nothing in the design says why the right fix is a gate edit rather than an object-level patch. Gate editing also does not mechanically remove stale edits; only revalidation does. Arm (c) could 'win' by loosening its gate or reward-hacking the new verifier, and stale-edit counts alone would not tell those apart from real adaptation. There are only about 9 real commit transitions in total (3 benchmarks x 3 steps). Most are narrow bug fixes, and a -5.66 pp commit on a 60-task subset touches roughly 3 to 4 tasks, so the 10% flip go-criterion is likely to fail. Fitting a tau-vs-|Delta| law on about 9 points with 5 lineages each is not credible. On scooping, tn4eslVXEK already owns the stale-progress and verifier-overfit metrics, so the novelty rests entirely on the behavioral gate-edit claim. · must fix: (1) Add a synthetic-drift control matched on |Delta| and on which tasks are affected, plus a stationary-grader arm with the gate editable, so that 'real drift causes meta edits' is identifiable and not just 'S5 is exposed' or 'any score drop'. (2) Add a diff/changelog-visible arm against a feedback-only arm. (3) Classify every gate edit by type (tightening, loosening, revalidation trigger) and report gate edits that hack the post-commit verifier, scored by the intended grader. Without that, a higher gate-edit rate is uninterpretable. (4) Get the BenchPulse commit list in week 1 and choose commits that span a wide |Delta| range, including sign flips. If fewer than about 6 usable transitions exist, drop the tau law to an exploratory appendix. (5) Budget the tau-bench user-simulator LLM calls and GPU rental for 5 arms x 5 lineages x 3 benchmarks x 4 states. This probably means cutting to 2 benchmarks and 3 core arms (a, c, plus synthetic) to stay within $3k, with a second open model (for example Llama or GLM) rather than only an API check. (6) Run a blind audit of a sample of edit attributions, because the S4/S5 classification drives the headline number.
  > "The use of real verifier commits as an exogenous shock is a clever natural experiment. But with roughly nine transitions, mostly small bug fixes, and no control for any score regression of matched size, I cannot tell whether the agent is responding to grader drift or simply to regressions. I also cannot tell whether a gate edit means adaptation or that the agent learned to lower its own bar. The metrics largely restate tn4eslVXEK at the harness level."
- **expert**: strongest: The causal question is concrete, falsifiable, and new: does exogenous, real grader maintenance push an agent to edit its own gate or improver? It speaks directly to the user's question about why meta-harnesses rarely update themselves. It is also grounded in reality, because it uses mined commits instead of synthetic drift, and that is a real point of difference from the co-evolution papers (Red Queen GM, Gödel Ecology), where the system controls its own evaluator. Arm (d), the oracle revalidation procedure, together with the shuffled-order control, gives a clean ceiling and a clean null reference. · weakest: The signal is likely too thin, and the main effect has confounds. (1) Most real verifier commits are small bug fixes. On 60 tasks, a -5.66 pp commit changes about 3 or 4 task verdicts, so "edits whose benefit flips sign" will be a handful of events per lineage. With 3 to 5 lineages, the negative-binomial GLM and the fitted exp(-t/τ) will be badly underpowered, and the τ-vs-|Δ| law rests on perhaps 3 to 4 commit points per benchmark with a narrow |Δ| range. (2) The gate-edit share is confounded by exposure and by reward hacking. An editable S5 under a moving grader can "reduce stale edits" just by loosening or resetting the gate, which inflates held-out-under-current-verifier scores without tracking the intended grader. You need a gate-edit-under-stationary-grader arm and a classification of gate edits (tighten, revalidate, loosen). (3) Qwen3-32B may never edit S5 in either condition. Then you get a 0% versus 0% null, which is a weak paper next to SelfImprove-Bench's existing 0% finding. (4) Scope is large for a solo undergrad: 3 benchmarks x 4 pinned verifier states x 5 arms x 5 lineages x 2 models, plus τ-bench user simulation, plus a human-validated intended grader. (5) Novelty hinges on tn4eslVXEK. Reviewers will read the paper as "tn4eslVXEK's evaluator-revision false-progress setup moved to a harness loop with BenchPulse data", which is borderline K4. · must fix: Make the headline the causal edit-rate claim and power it properly. Pick commits from BenchPulse's census that are high-|Δ| or flip signs, aiming for at least 15% task-verdict churn. Use at least 150 tasks per benchmark, or stack several commits. Run at least 8 lineages for the arms that matter, (a), (c), and a stationary-grader gate-editable arm (c0), and cut the other arms and benchmarks to one benchmark plus one replication. Add a synthetic-drift control with matched |Δ| and a changelog-visible versus feedback-only arm. Code every gate edit as tighten, revalidate, or loosen, and report the intended-grader score as the primary outcome so that gate loosening cannot count as a win. Pre-register the τ law as secondary. Cite tn4eslVXEK explicitly and frame your setup as its extension to harness-level, exogenous, real-commit drift.
  > "The question of whether real grader drift induces meta-level self-modification is interesting and under-explored. However, only a handful of tasks actually change verdict per commit, and the gate-editable arm can reduce 'stale edits' by simply relaxing its acceptance criteria. Without a stationary-grader gate-editable control and edit-type coding, I cannot distinguish adaptive revalidation from gate erosion. The proposed decay law is fit to too few commit events to be meaningful. Relative to 'When the Evaluator Changes' (tn4eslVXEK), the contribution reads as a port to a harness loop. Weak reject, would raise with a properly powered single-benchmark study."
- **mentor**: strongest: This tests the user's question directly: does real, outside pressure, not a designed curriculum, make a self-improver change its own meta-level? It does this with actual mined grader commits, which is concrete and hard to dismiss. As far as the scoop check found, no prior work treats exogenous verifier churn as the stream for a harness self-improver with an editable gate. That puts it squarely in Yin's Gödel Agent territory, and the cross-version framing gives a natural extension of tn4eslVXEK. · weakest: The mechanism is underspecified, and the obvious outcome is a boring null. If every gate decision already re-scores candidates under the current verifier, a frozen gate is never 'stale'. Staleness only exists if earlier accepted edits are never re-checked, and then the oracle arm (d) is the whole answer while arm (c) depends on the LLM spontaneously writing revalidation logic. SelfImprove-Bench already reports 0% improver edits. So '~0% gate edits even under drift' is the likely result. It is publishable only as a minor confirmation, not as a resolution of the 'bug or artefact' debate. Real verifier commits are mostly small bug fixes: the |Δ| range is narrow and there are only about 4 events per benchmark. That makes the exp(-t/τ) law and the |Δ|-scaling claim close to impossible to estimate. Then there is scale: 5 arms × 5 lineages × 4 states × 3 benchmarks, with Qwen3-32B as both agent and τ-bench user simulator, on a modest GPU. That is well beyond what one undergrad can run in 6 months. · must fix: (1) Before the pilot, pin down the gate semantics. What exactly is in S5? Does the gate keep cached scores for retained edits? What can a gate edit actually change: revalidation, thresholds, or which verifier signals it reads? Show that a frozen gate really carries stale edits forward; otherwise the stale-edit metric is zero by construction. (2) Cut to 1–2 benchmarks and 3 arms: frozen gate, gate-editable, and a gate-editable arm under a stationary grader (this rules out the explanation that S5 is simply more exposed). Add one synthetic-drift control with matched |Δ|; drop shuffled-order and the multi-benchmark breadth. (3) Add the diff/changelog-visible arm. It is the cheapest way to get a positive result, and it separates 'notices drift' from 'can act on drift'. (4) Make the headline the causal edit-rate claim. Report τ only as a descriptive, pre-registered secondary result, and choose commits with sign flips so |Δ| varies. (5) Budget the τ-bench user simulator explicitly. Use a smaller local simulator or a cached API simulator, and check that $3k covers 2 models × lineages.
  > "The question is interesting: does real benchmark maintenance push self-improvers to edit their own acceptance criteria? But the paper never makes clear why a gate that already scores against the current verifier would hold stale edits in the first place, and the oracle revalidation arm suggests the fix is a two-line procedure, not emergent meta-learning. With four commits per benchmark, the decay law is effectively a fit through four points, and the near-zero gate-edit rate largely restates SelfImprove-Bench."

### The Evolvability Window: Meta-Level Self-Modification Pays Off Only at Intermediate Rates of Real-World Change (a Modularly-Varying-Goals Lens) (`reality-evolvability-switching-period`) — panel 65.8/100 ★ non-obvious

**Claim.** Borrowing modularly varying goals from evolutionary biology (Kashtan & Alon 2005, where evolvability evolves only under periodic goal switching that shares subgoals), the claim is that both the frequency of improver-level self-edits and their payoff relative to a fixed improver peak at an intermediate era-switching period T* of about 2 to 4 improver generations. The prediction is an inverted U: the payoff is about zero for very fast switching (noise) and for very slow switching (effectively stationary), and is clearly positive at T*.

**If the opposite holds.** Finding that payoff rises monotonically with switching frequency would say that 'more change, more meta-learning' with no fast-switch penalty, so benchmarks should simply maximize churn. Finding that payoff is flat would say that the timescale of the environment does not explain why improvers rarely self-edit. Either result gives harness and benchmark designers a quantitative design rule, and the biology mapping is novel in this literature.

**Law.** Payoff(T) ∝ (T/τ_g) · exp(−T/T_s), an inverted U. τ_g is the improver's generation time and T_s is the mean persistence of a meta-rule's usefulness. The peak is at T* = T_s. The prediction is that T* is near 2–4 generations under shared-subgoal (modular) switching, and that there is no interior peak under non-modular switching.

**Design.** Testbed: the shared Gödel-lite loop with S1–S5, comparing editable-improver arms against fixed-improver arms.

Environment: the real-drift eras from DriftEras (API drift types E1–E3), plus a goal component made of two real task families that share subskills (for example pydantic-based validation and httpx client code over the same real schemas, which is the 'modular' shared structure). The schedule alternates between era states with period T ∈ {0.5, 1, 2, 4, 8, ∞} improver generations, where one generation is one fixed batch of tasks and one improvement step. Reverting changes are included (deprecation, then shim restored), so retention matters.

A modularity control: alternate between two unrelated drift sources that share no subgoals. Biology predicts no evolvability peak in this case.

Matched total compute per lineage. Models: Qwen3-14B and 32B. Six lineages per T per arm (two arms × six T × two modularity conditions).

Metrics:
- improver-edit rate;
- adaptation lag after each switch (generations needed to recover 90% of pre-switch accuracy), which is the biological 'evolvability' readout;
- final held-out accuracy on an unseen fifth era.
Statistics: fit a quadratic in log T and alternatively a piecewise model. The preregistered test is a negative quadratic coefficient with the vertex inside the grid, using the two-lines test (Simonsohn) for the U shape.

**Pilot.** Use T ∈ {1, 4, ∞}, the modular condition only, 3 lineages on Qwen3-14B, 2 libraries, and about $20 for drafting tasks (reusing the DriftEras pilot).
Go if the improver-editable arm's lag reduction at T = 4 exceeds the reductions at T = 1 and T = ∞ by at least 1 generation in at least 2 of 3 lineages.
Kill if lag is identical across T (CI overlap at every T). In that case the timescale lens does not bite and should be dropped.

**Closest work (author's view).** Kashtan & Alon 2005 (PNAS, 'Spontaneous evolution of modularity and network motifs') shows evolvability arising under switching goals, but for biological and neural networks rather than LLM improvers. The Seven-Year Itch 2e5z57jIPK finds a crossover in the length of memory history, not in the switching period of the environment. Continual-ARC 2611.14505 tests dormant return but is stationary and object-level. Metaⁿ 2608.24735 finds that depth stalls, but in stationary settings. SelfImprove-Bench 2611.02797 gets 0% improver edits without forcing. None varies the environment's switching period to test when meta-level change pays.

**Scoop check: open.** I found no LLM prior or concurrent work that sweeps the environment's switching period and compares editable against fixed improvers. The design's core is unclaimed. Three changes would help it survive review:

(1) Biology already owns the inverted-U itself. Kashtan & Alon 2005/2007, Stephens 1991 and Dunlap & Stephens 2009 (learning is favoured at intermediate rates of environmental change) and Bedau & Packard 2003 (evolution of evolvability via mutation rates) all have it. Frame the paper as a test of whether LLM meta-level self-modification obeys that law. The new parts are the measured T* in improver generations and the modular-vs-non-modular dissociation. Cite Lo & Zhao 2611.07048 as the single-shift theory you extend to periodic switching.

(2) Use 2610.25858 as the stream/metric precedent and as a baseline. Its blocked vs interleaved Mix results are the two T endpoints of your sweep. Reuse its plasticity/stability/interference decomposition so adaptation lag is comparable.

(3) Handle the empty-cell risk before anything else. SelfImprove-Bench reports 0% organic improver edits, and Improvement by Inheritance agrees. Run a ≤$50 pilot at T = 2 with Qwen3-32B and measure the organic improver-edit rate. If it is near zero, change the headline from 'edit frequency peaks at T*' to 'payoff of a forced or budgeted improver edit peaks at T*'. Under that design, every lineage must make one improver edit every k generations, and you measure its marginal value against a fixed improver. Report the organic edit rate as a secondary outcome: 'organic self-edit rate does not track T*' is a publishable negative result in its own right.

Also:
- Make DriftEras secondary. PACE-Bench, SkillShift, MCPEvol and EvoHarnessBench crowd the drift-benchmark space, so the contribution has to be the law, not the benchmark.
- Six lineages per cell may be too few for a Simonsohn two-lines test on a noisy payoff. Power the test from the pilot, or cut the T grid to {0.5, 1, 2, 4, ∞} and add lineages.

| Paper | Title | Summary | Overlap |
|---|---|---|---|
| arXiv:2610.25858 | Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners? (Mila/GDM) | Runs ReasoningBank, GEPA and Meta-Harness on four blocked drifting streams (topic, workflow, domain, compound) built from BIRD/BFCL/ScienceWorld. Plasticity and stability trade off (r = -0.72). Includes an interleaved Mix schedule as a stationary reference. Meta-Harness's gate freezes the workspace, and BFCL-Miss to BFCL-Base produces a behavioral reversal. | Medium. It is the nearest LLM-side neighbour for drifting streams and the closest thing to a switching-period manipulation (blocked vs fully interleaved, i.e. T at two extremes). It never sweeps T, never edits the improver, and has no fixed-vs-editable-improver contrast, no modular-vs-non-modular control and no inverted-U claim. Use it as the stream/metric precedent and as a baseline (the Mix result is effectively your T to 0 limit). |
| arXiv:2611.07048 | Adapting the Process of Adaptation: Darwinian, Lamarckian, and Agent-Directed Evolution in AI (Lo & Zhao, MIT, Nov 2026) | Theory paper using the Brennan-Lo growth-regret framework. It compares adaptation time and rate after one permanent environmental shift for Darwinian, Lamarckian and agent-directed evolution, gives an optimal number of variants N*, and covers misspecified targets. The only experiments are Monte Carlo simulations. | Low-medium. It is the closest theoretical framing of 'when does directing your own adaptation pay off after environmental change'. It has a single permanent shift, no periodic switching, no meta-rule persistence T_s, no inverted U and no LLM experiments. Cite it as the theory foil; your Payoff(T) law extends it to periodic switching. |
| arXiv:2608.24735 | Meta^n: Recursive Self-Improvement through Emergent Depth | Recursive meta-levels for LLM self-improvement. Depth stalls in stationary settings. | Low. Stationary only. Its forward citations (Dream-RSI 2609.14858, HMED 2610.07979, Auditing the Product 2610.27610, Relative-Computability 2605.27381, Dalek, SanSi, Science or Slop) contain no switching-period study. |
| arXiv:2611.02797 | SelfImprove-Bench | 87.9% of retained edits touch prompts or memory, and 0% touch the improver unless forced. | Low on the claim, high on feasibility risk. Your improver-edit-rate metric may be zero in every cell. Forward citations (2611.14256 survey, 2611.11342 failure traces) do not vary environmental change rates. |
| arXiv:2606.26173 | AlgoEvolve: LLM-driven Meta-evolution of Algorithmic Trading Programs | Bi-level setup: an outer loop evolves the evolver prompt while the inner loop evolves strategies on non-stationary intraday markets. It reports an anecdotal regime shift at MG3 followed by a 'diversity injection' pivot. | Low. It is anecdotal evidence that meta-evolution helps after a regime shift. Only 6 meta-generations, no rate sweep, no controlled fixed-improver arm across T, no inverted U. |
| arXiv:2609.14858 | Dream-RSI: Recursive Self-Improvement through Evolving Worlds | Meta-layer RSI over exploration policies using discovery history as replay simulators. Compared against Recursive Fixed Exploration (fixed meta-policy). | Low. It does have a fixed-vs-editable meta-policy contrast, but tasks are stationary. 'Evolving worlds' refers to a growing pool of simulators, not environmental change. |
| OpenReview:0b4wysHrzh | Beyond Self-Evolution: Meta-Evolving Agents for Cross-Task Agent Improvement (MetaEvoForge, ICLR27 sub) | Evolved vs unevolved improvers across 62 environments, tested on transfer to held-out suites. | Low. It has an editable-vs-fixed improver contrast, but across task families, not across switching rates of one changing environment. |
| arXiv:2608.14441 / 2610.33528 / 2607.14642 / 2609.04280 | Drift benchmarks: PACE-Bench (physics change), SkillShift-Bench (web env evolution), MCPEvol-Bench (MCP server evolution), EvoHarnessBench (evolving harness) | Benchmarks that test whether self-evolving agents recover after their environment changes. | Low on the claim. They crowd the 'real drift benchmark' contribution, so DriftEras alone will not count as novel. None varies switching period or tests the improver level. |
| OpenReview:hip2PfZSRg | Do Neural Networks Lose Plasticity in a Gradually Changing World? (ICLR27 sub) | Plasticity loss depends on how abrupt task transitions are (weight-level NN). | Low. It is a rate-of-change manipulation, but on abruptness and for weights, not meta-level payoff vs switching period. |

**Judge panel**

| Lens | N | S | R | C | F | T | Total | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ac | 3 | 2.5 | 4.5 | 2.5 | 2.5 | 3.5 | 60.8 | pursue-with-fixes |
| expert | 3.5 | 3.5 | 4 | 3 | 2.5 | 4 | 68.3 | pursue-with-fixes |
| mentor | 3.5 | 3.5 | 4 | 3 | 2.5 | 4 | 68.3 | pursue-with-fixes |

- **ac**: strongest: This is the only design I know of that manipulates the environment's switching period as an independent variable and contrasts an editable improver against a fixed one. It gives a direct, mechanistic answer to the mentor's question: why do meta-harnesses rarely update themselves, and when should they? The modular vs non-modular control is the one piece that could turn 'there is a peak' into a real dissociation with a theory behind it. · weakest: The inverted U is close to guaranteed by construction, so the headline result is not very surprising. With T=infinity there is no change to adapt to, and with T=0.5 the eras switch inside a single batch, so no edit made per generation can follow them. Payoff near zero at both ends falls out of the definitions, not out of any law. The proposed law Payoff ∝ (T/τ_g)·exp(−T/T_s) adds nothing as written: T_s is never measured independently, so 'T* = T_s' is fitted after the fact and cannot be falsified. Two further problems: SelfImprove-Bench reports 0% organic improver edits, so the edit-rate DV may be empty in every cell, and the forced-edit fallback changes the claim. Power is also weak: 6 lineages in a two-lines test on a noisy payoff. And the full grid of 144 lineages, where T=8 needs roughly 40 or more generations per lineage, with Qwen3-32B in the mix, is unlikely to fit within $3k of GPU time for a solo undergrad. · must fix: (1) Measure T_s on its own, before the sweep. One way: take improver edits made in era A, replay them frozen, and record how many generations they keep a positive marginal value. Then preregister T* = T_s as an out-of-sample prediction. That makes the law falsifiable rather than a curve fit. (2) Make the modular vs non-modular dissociation the headline test (a T × modularity interaction), not the U shape itself, because the U is mostly guaranteed by the extreme cells. Drop T=0.5, or justify it as a separate within-batch-mixing condition tied to 2610.25858. (3) Run the ≤$50 organic-edit pilot first. If organic edits are near zero, change the main DV to the marginal value of a forced or budgeted improver edit, and report the organic edit rate as a secondary negative result. (4) Make the cost credible. Use 14B only for the sweep and 32B only for a replication at T* and at the two endpoints, with GPU-hour accounting. Power the lineage count from the pilot's variance, aiming for at least 10 per key cell, and use a smaller grid such as {1, 2, 4, 8, ∞}. (5) Match compute per generation and per lineage across T, and use the same number of switch events, not only the same total compute. Otherwise lineages at large T see fewer switches and the adaptation-lag estimate becomes noisier at that end.
  > "The authors find that meta-level self-modification pays off at intermediate switching rates. But that is nearly true by definition: no edit can track switches faster than a generation, and nothing needs tracking in a stationary environment. The paper's own contribution would have to be the predicted location of the peak and the modular/non-modular dissociation. The first is fitted after the fact through an unmeasured T_s, and the second rests on six lineages per cell. I would want T_s estimated independently and the interaction properly powered before I believe the 'law' is anything more than a parameterised curve fit."
- **expert**: strongest: The core manipulation is unclaimed and clean. It sweeps the environment's switching period and compares an editable improver against a fixed one, with a modular vs non-modular control. That gives a dissociation test with a sharp, preregisterable shape prediction (vertex inside the grid, plus Simonsohn two-lines). It also speaks directly to the user's two questions, 'why don't meta-harnesses update themselves' and 'what kind of continual, grounded benchmark forces them to', with a timescale answer rather than another benchmark. The biology framing (Kashtan & Alon, Stephens 1991, Dunlap & Stephens 2009) gives reviewers an established prior to test against, not a vague analogy. 2610.25858's blocked vs Mix results anchor the endpoints for free. · weakest: Three structural problems.

(a) The empty-cell problem. Organic improver-edit rate is about 0% (SelfImprove-Bench, Improvement-by-Inheritance). So the 'edit-frequency peaks at T*' headline is probably unmeasurable, and the forced or budgeted-edit fallback changes the claim to something weaker.

(b) The 'law' is close to unfalsifiable as written. Payoff ∝ (T/τ_g)·exp(−T/T_s), with T* = T_s fit from the same curve, is circular unless T_s (meta-rule usefulness persistence) is measured independently, for example by re-applying a frozen meta-edit k generations later. The predicted T* of 2–4 generations sits in the middle of a 6-point log grid, so almost any hump 'confirms' it.

(c) Compute and power. 2 arms × 6 T × 2 modularity × 6 lineages × 2 models is about 288 lineages. The T=8 arm needs at least 3–4 periods, so 30+ generations each, on 14B/32B agentic coding loops. That is very tight for $3k and an undergrad. Six noisy lineages per cell is underpowered for a U test. 'Modular' (pydantic+httpx sharing schemas) vs 'non-modular' is a hand-built distinction that reviewers will call arbitrary unless shared-subskill overlap is quantified.

The T=∞ ≈ 0 payoff prediction also conflicts with DGM/ADAS-style stationary gains. If stationary meta-edits help even modestly, the curve is monotone-decreasing, not a U. · must fix: 1. Before anything else, run the ≤$50 organic-edit-rate pilot at T=2 on Qwen3-32B. If the rate is near 0, make the primary outcome the marginal payoff of a scheduled improver edit (one every k generations) vs a fixed improver, and report organic edit rate as a secondary outcome.

2. Measure T_s independently of the payoff curve. Freeze each meta-edit and re-test its benefit at lags 1..8 generations to get a persistence half-life, then preregister T* = T_s as an out-of-sample prediction. That turns curve-fitting into a test.

3. Cut the design to roughly 2 arms × {0.5,1,2,4,∞} × 2 modularity × 10+ lineages on one model (14B). Use 32B only for the T* and T=∞ replication cells. Power the two-lines test from the pilot variance.

4. Make modularity graded, not binary. Use a measured shared-subskill overlap fraction (for example the fraction of shared test-relevant API symbols), so the prediction is that peak height scales with overlap.

5. Make DriftEras explicitly secondary to the law, and reuse the 2610.25858 plasticity/stability/interference decomposition so adaptation lag is comparable to prior work.
  > "The question of when meta-level self-modification pays off as a function of the environment's switching period is new for LLM self-improvers, and the modular/non-modular control is a nice touch borrowed from Kashtan & Alon. However, the 'law' is fit to the same data it claims to predict. T_s is never measured independently. The organic improver-edit rate is near zero in the authors' own setting, so the headline quantity becomes a forced-edit intervention. With six lineages per cell I am not convinced the inverted-U survives proper multiple-comparison control. I would raise my score if T* were predicted out-of-sample from a separately measured meta-rule persistence and the modularity effect were graded rather than binary."
- **mentor**: strongest: The modular-vs-non-modular switching control. It turns a curve-fitting exercise into a dissociation test with a mechanism: shared-subgoal switching should produce an interior T* and unrelated switching should not. No LLM self-improvement paper sweeps the environment's switching period against a fixed-improver vs editable-improver contrast. It also fits Yin's Gödel Agent line directly, because it asks when self-referential edits to the improver are worth making at all. · weakest: Two problems: the outcome may not exist, and the budget does not hold. SelfImprove-Bench and Improvement-by-Inheritance both report roughly 0% organic improver edits, so the headline metric (edit rate peaking at T*) will probably be zero in every cell. Forcing edits changes the claim to 'value of a forced meta-edit vs T', which is a weaker and less biological claim. The 'law' Payoff ∝ (T/τ_g)·exp(−T/T_s) is close to circular: its peak is at T_s by construction, and T_s is not measured independently. Compute is also badly underestimated. The design is 2 arms × 6 T × 2 modularity × 6 lineages × 2 models = 288 lineages. Each T = 8 lineage needs at least 3 switches (about 24+ generations), and every generation is a batch of agentic coding tasks plus an improver step. That is far beyond a modest GPU for 32B, and very tight at $3k even with cheap hosted Qwen. With 6 noisy lineages per cell, the Simonsohn two-lines test will probably be underpowered. · must fix: (1) Before anything else, run a ≤$50 organic-edit pilot at T = 2. If the organic edit rate is below about 5%, preregister the forced/budgeted-edit design (one improver edit every k generations, marginal value against a fixed improver) as the primary outcome, and report the organic edit rate as a secondary result. (2) Estimate T_s independently, as the measured half-life of a meta-rule's usefulness from rollback/ablation of past improver edits. Then predict T* from it before running the sweep, so the law can actually fail. Otherwise drop the functional form and preregister only the inverted U plus the modular dissociation. (3) Cut the scope. Use one model (Qwen3-14B) for the full grid and run 32B only at T ∈ {1, T*, ∞} as a replication. Use the grid T ∈ {0.5, 1, 2, 4, ∞} and power the lineages per cell from the pilot variance; expect 10+. Put a cost spreadsheet in the plan. (4) Frame the paper as testing whether LLM meta-level self-modification obeys the known biological law (Kashtan & Alon; Stephens 1991; Dunlap & Stephens 2009). Use 2610.25858 blocked/interleaved as the T endpoints and baseline, and reuse its plasticity/stability decomposition. Make DriftEras a secondary contribution.
  > "The modular-vs-non-modular dissociation is a clever import from evolutionary biology, and I have not seen it tested on LLM improvers. However, the stated 'law' peaks at T_s by construction and T_s is never measured independently. Most cells report improver edits that had to be forced. As a result, the paper shows at most that a mandated meta-edit's value depends weakly on switching period in one testbed with six seeds, which falls short of a law."

### DriftEras: Real Library-History Eras Whose Drift *Type* Changes Make Improver Self-Edits Necessary, Not Optional (`reality-era-typed-drift`) — panel 65.7/100

**Claim.** On a stream built from real post-cutoff Python library releases, where each era brings a different drift type (deprecation with a warning shim, then silent semantic change, then hard removal), a Gödel-lite loop that may edit its own improver procedure ends with at least 10 pp higher held-out success than a matched-compute fixed-improver loop. On a control stream that uses the same real changes but keeps one drift type throughout, the gap is under 2 pp.

**If the opposite holds.** If the improver-editable arm does not beat the fixed improver even when the drift type switches, the 'improvers never self-edit' finding (SelfImprove-Bench 0%, Improvement by Inheritance) is a capability gap, not an incentive gap. That would be the first real-data evidence for it, and it changes what the field should build: improver-edit training or scaffolds rather than better benchmarks. Either outcome ships a reusable, time-split, certified real-drift benchmark, which the review shows does not exist.

**Law.** Predicted interaction: Δ(c − b) ≈ β · (number of drift-type switches seen), with β > 0 on the type-switching stream and β ≈ 0 on the control stream. Secondary prediction: improver-edit rate rises with stale-rule harm in the preceding era.

**Design.** Shared testbed: a Gödel-lite / Meta-Harness-lite loop with five editable surfaces, enabled per arm: S1 task prompt, S2 memory/notes, S3 tool wrappers, S4 improver prompt and procedure (how failures are diagnosed and edits proposed), S5 acceptance gate code.

Data: about 8 libraries with heavy churn and releases after the model's cutoff (e.g. pydantic, httpx, pandas, the openai SDK, langchain-core, numpy 2.x, sqlalchemy, polars). Release diffs and changelogs come from Bridge/LibEvoBench-style mining, and each version is pinned in Docker.

Tasks: about 60 per era, in four eras (E0 baseline, E1 deprecation with shim, E2 silent semantic change, E3 removal). Each task is a short coding task with hidden tests, LLM-drafted from the changelog. Each is certified EnvOS-style: the reference solution passes on the new version, the old-era idiom fails or diverges, and the task is solvable. Each era is split 40/20 into train and held-out tasks.

Arms, all at a matched token budget per era:
(a) S1–S2 only;
(b) S1–S3;
(c) S1–S5 (Gödel-lite);
(d) S1–S5 plus a forced-edit control (one improver edit per era, SelfImprove-Bench style);
(e) full-context ICL baseline (the CL-Bench winner).
Control stream: the same real changes regrouped so every era mixes drift types uniformly, which removes the type switch.

Models: Qwen3-32B or Qwen3-Coder-30B-A3B served on vLLM, plus one cheap API model as a robustness check. Five lineages per arm per stream, with web isolation.

Metrics:
- held-out success per era and at the end of the stream;
- forward transfer of improvement ability: checkpoint the improver at the end of era k, reset the object-level state, run it on era k+1 and compare against a generation-0 improver;
- improver-edit rate;
- stale-rule harm rate.

Statistics: mixed-effects logistic regression (arm × stream × era, random effects for lineage and task), with the arm×stream interaction as the preregistered test and bootstrap CIs.

**Pilot.** Use 2 libraries (pydantic v1→v2→v2.x semantic changes, httpx), 3 eras, 25 tasks per era, arms (a) and (c), 3 lineages, Qwen3-32B local plus at most $40 of API calls for task drafting.
Go if both hold: (1) at least 70% of drafted tasks pass certification, and the old-era idiom fails on at least 80% of certified tasks; (2) in E2 the fixed-improver arm keeps applying an E1 rule that is now wrong on at least 20% of tasks. Without (2), object-level adaptation is not being stressed.
Kill if (c) never edits S4/S5 even after the E2 failures and also does not beat (a), and a manual check shows the S4 edit interface itself works. In that case pivot to the capability-gap framing.

**Closest work (author's view).** Δτ-Bench 2610.34238 uses synthetic, capability-preserving tool transforms and only object-level skill methods; the delta here is real releases plus a change in drift type. Are-SE-CL 2610.25858 builds synthetic block streams with a fixed evolver; the delta is that the improver itself varies. MemDev-Bench 2610.27524 has a real, time-valid pipeline but is memory-only, with no improver and no change in drift type. SelfImprove-Bench 2611.02797 forces improver edits but on stationary tasks; the delta is environment pressure rather than a forced arm. LibEvoBench 2606.25402 and Bridge 2608.30497 use real API evolution for one-shot evaluation only.

**Scoop check: medium-risk.** I found no paper that makes this exact claim: that switching drift type on a real library-release stream is the condition under which letting the improver edit itself beats a fixed improver, with the arm×stream interaction as the preregistered test. Every component has a close neighbour, though. The paper needs repositioning to survive:

(1) Present it as "when does editing the improver pay?", an environmental-necessity account that competes with VERSE's verification account. Cross S4 with verification availability (with/without execution replay) in a 2x2, so the result separates non-stationarity from verification as the cause.

(2) Cite Are-SE-CL as the source of the stream design, including its compound and Mix control. The new parts are the S4 arm, real post-cutoff releases, and the drift-type variable. Do not claim "drift-typed streams" or the "mixed control" as contributions.

(3) Add Harness^2-style harnessing-level recursion and a MetaEvoForge-style evolved improver as baseline arms. The forward-transfer-of-improver metric is then a test across time-ordered eras, not across tasks.

(4) Take the drift taxonomy from ReScopeBench and AgentDriftBench, and the silent-semantic-change task source from Update from Hell. Measure stale-rule harm as ReScopeBench measures update coverage.

(5) The biggest empirical risk: improvers rarely edit themselves (SelfImprove-Bench, Improvement by Inheritance). The 10 pp target is aggressive. Pilot one switch (E1 to E2) first. Preregister a smaller effect plus the edit-rate prediction (improver edits rise after stale-rule harm). That prediction is the most novel and least scooped piece, so consider leading with it if the accuracy gap is small.

(6) Drop the "law" framing unless at least 3 switch counts are tested. With four eras there are at most 3 switches, so β is close to unidentifiable.

| Paper | Title | Summary | Overlap |
|---|---|---|---|
| arXiv 2610.25858 (Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners?, Mila/GDM) | Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners? | Runs ReasoningBank, GEPA and Meta-Harness on four drifting streams (topic, workflow, domain, compound) built from BIRD, BFCL and ScienceWorld. Plasticity trades off against stability. App. A has an interleaved 'Mix' stream as a stationary control, with the same tasks uniformly mixed. | High on the stream design: it already has drift typed by kind, a compound stream that switches drift type, and a regrouped mixed control, which is structurally the same as DriftEras' control stream. The evolver is always fixed, there is no arm that edits the improver, and the streams are benchmark-family shifts rather than real library releases. Reviewers will call DriftEras 'Are-SE-CL plus an S4 arm on SE data'. |
| OpenReview s3UU3VSGcB / arXiv 2610.14224 | Harness^2: Recursive Agent Harnessing for an Open World | Two levels of recursion. Task-level edits refine the harness. A harnessing-level recursion pulls experience out of those edits to update a continual improvement harness, so the system adapts to future novel tasks. | Medium-high on the mechanism: an improver that updates itself across a sequence of novel tasks, and it helps. It does not manipulate drift type, has no matched fixed-improver arm under switch vs no-switch streams, and does not use real API evolution. Must be cited as the existing method of 'improver adapts across tasks'. Possibly a baseline arm. |
| OpenReview 9WwpxPfcji / arXiv 2610.02616 | VERSE: Verified Self-Evolving Optimizer for Agent Harnesses | Studies whether the optimizer should also evolve its own diagnosis, edit and test procedures. In a controlled study, optimizer self-evolution helps only when execution-based verification is available. | Medium-high on the S4 'improver self-edit helps' claim, but on stationary tasks. It gives a competing explanation (verification, not environment non-stationarity, decides when improver edits pay), which DriftEras must control for by crossing S4 with verification availability. |
| OpenReview 0b4wysHrzh | Beyond Self-Evolution: Meta-Evolving Agents for Cross-Task Agent Improvement (MetaEvoForge) | An independent MetaAgent improver evolves its improvement policy from 768 improvement pairs across 62 environments. Evolved improvers are compared with unevolved ones on held-out suites at a fixed budget, and gains depend on the task. | Medium on DriftEras' 'forward transfer of improvement ability' metric (evolved vs generation-0 improver on the next era). That is already done across tasks, but not across time-ordered drift eras. |

Tracks whether memory is revised or goes stale, and reports performance by change mechanism. | Medium: it has typed silent changes, stale-experience measurement and per-mechanism analysis. It is memory/skill only (S2), has no improver, and its environments are simulated rather than real library releases. |
| arXiv 2611.03219 | Can Coding Agents Sustain Continual Evolution without Forgetting? | Runs AHE and Meta-Harness continually across SWE-Bench, SWT-Bench and FeatureBench. It finds catastrophic forgetting and sorts harness conflicts into verification, workflow and budget types. | Medium-low: continual harness evolution for coding agents with a fixed evolver. The shifts are task types, not drift types in a library. Useful as a baseline and for its analysis taxonomy. |
| OpenReview 5xFWggnePr | AgentDriftBench | Injects five drift families (renamed params, new required fields, deprecated tools, and others) into tool-use episodes. CATE and TASL: object-level methods for evolving tool and API interfaces. | Low: each covers the data side ('silent semantic change' in real upgrades) or the object-level adaptation methods.
