**Judge panel**

| Lens | N | S | R | C | F | T | Total | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ac | 2.5 | 3.5 | 4.5 | 2.5 | 3 | 4 | 64.6 | pursue-with-fixes (K4) |
| expert | 3 | 3.5 | 4 | 3 | 2.5 | 3.5 | 65 | pursue-with-fixes |
| mentor | 3 | 3.5 | 4.5 | 3 | 2.5 | 4 | 67.5 | pursue-with-fixes (K4) |

- **ac**: strongest: The arm×stream interaction is a clean, preregisterable test of a question the field keeps arguing about: is 'improvers rarely self-edit' an incentive gap or a capability gap? Either answer is informative. The secondary prediction (improver-edit rate rises after stale-rule harm) is the least scooped part and is mechanistically interesting. The certified, time-split benchmark of real library drift is a reusable artifact. · weakest: The core manipulation is confounded and the novelty is thin. (1) Drift type is tied to era order and version recency: E3 'removal' tasks are always later, newer and harder, and the regrouped control changes both the difficulty mix and how often drift switches (a uniform mix means more frequent local switches, not fewer). So 'no type switch' is not a clean null condition. (2) 'Post-cutoff' fails for the pilot itself: pydantic v1→v2 (2023) and httpx are well inside Qwen3's training data, so the old idiom may not be what the model defaults to, and that kills Go criterion (2). (3) S4/S5 arms get extra diagnosis and verification compute in practice even at a 'matched token budget', which is exactly VERSE's competing explanation. (4) Structurally this is Are-SE-CL's typed/compound/mixed stream design plus an S4 arm on SE data, i.e. K4-adjacent. (5) With at most 3 switches and 5 lineages, the 'law' β cannot be identified, and power for a 2 pp vs 10 pp interaction with ~20 held-out tasks per era per library is marginal. · must fix: Replace the era-ordered design with a counterbalanced one, so drift-type order is permuted across lineages (e.g. Latin square over E1/E2/E3) and the 'switch' effect is not confounded with recency or difficulty. Define the control as the same tasks in a single-type block of equal length and difficulty (difficulty-matched by fixed-improver pass rate), not a uniform mix. Use only releases after the model's training cutoff (verify with a no-docs probe that the model's default idiom is the old one), and drop pydantic v1→v2 from the pilot. Cross S4 with verification availability (2x2) to rule out VERSE's account, and log and match actual improver-side compute. Run a power analysis for the interaction before committing: probably fewer arms (drop d or e), more held-out tasks per era, and ≥8 lineages. Drop the 'law' framing. Lead with the edit-rate-after-harm prediction if the accuracy gap is small. Cut scope to 4 libraries.
  > ""This is Are-SE-CL's compound-versus-mixed stream with an improver-editing arm attached, and the drift-type 'switch' is confounded with era recency and task difficulty. Until order is counterbalanced and the libraries are verifiably post-cutoff for the model being tested, a positive interaction can't be attributed to drift type rather than to E2/E3 simply being harder. A null doesn't separate capability from incentive either, because a 32B improver may just be too weak. I'd like the edit-rate-after-harm analysis, but I can't accept the main claim as designed.""
- **expert**: strongest: It asks a clean causal question: does environmental non-stationarity, specifically a change in drift type, make improver self-edits pay? It has a preregistered arm x stream interaction test and a forced-edit control. This goes directly at the field's open puzzle of why improvers almost never self-edit (SelfImprove-Bench 0%, Improvement by Inheritance). The secondary prediction, that improver-edit rate rises after stale-rule harm, is the least scooped and most mechanistic piece. The real, post-cutoff, certified drift benchmark would be reusable either way. · weakest: The stream design (typed drift, a compound type-switching stream, a mixed regrouped control) is already in Are-SE-CL. Reviewers will read the paper as 'Are-SE-CL plus an S4 arm on library data', so the novelty rests entirely on the S4 result. The statistics are underpowered: 20 held-out tasks per era x 5 lineages cannot reliably detect a 10 pp interaction, let alone a 2 pp null band. The 'law' has at most 3 switch counts, so beta is unidentifiable. VERSE's verification account is an uncontrolled confound. The full grid is likely too large for one undergrad on <=$3k. That grid is 5+ arms x 2 streams x 4 eras x 5 lineages, plus the Harness^2 and MetaEvoForge baselines, plus certifying about 240 tasks across 8 Docker-pinned libraries, all on a 32B model that needs A100-class GPUs. The most probable outcome is that the editable arm rarely edits S4 and the gap is about 0. That is only publishable if the forced-edit arm and the edit-interface check cleanly separate a capability gap from an incentive gap. · must fix: (1) Lead with the edit-rate-after-stale-rule-harm mechanism, and frame the accuracy gap as 'when does editing the improver pay?'. Cross S4 with verification availability (a 2x2 with VERSE's account) so that non-stationarity and verification are separated as causes. (2) Credit Are-SE-CL for the switch and mix stream design, and claim only three things: the S4 variable, the real post-cutoff data, and certification. (3) Cut the scope to 3-4 libraries and 3 arms: fixed improver, editable improver, and forced-edit. The ICL baseline can be one cheap run. Raise held-out tasks to 40 or more per era, or pool across eras. Do a power analysis before committing to 10 pp, and replace the '<2 pp' claim with an equivalence test using a stated margin. (4) Drop the beta 'law' unless the switch count is varied (0/1/2/3) across many short streams. (5) Prove in the pilot that the old-era idiom actually fails for the base model, i.e. that the model is not already v2-fluent. Qwen3 has seen pydantic v2, so pydantic v1 to v2 is likely contaminated; choose releases after mid-2025. (6) Add an oracle-improver-edit arm, where a human-written S4 edit is injected, to bound the achievable gain.
  > "The question is well posed and the forced-edit control is a good idea. But the stream design is Are-SE-CL's, the 'improver adapts' mechanism is Harness^2's, and with 20 held-out tasks per era the claimed 10 pp interaction is within noise. If the editable arm never edits itself, as prior work predicts, the paper amounts to a benchmark plus a null result whose capability-gap interpretation is not convincingly isolated from interface or prompting failures. Weak accept if the oracle-edit and verification controls are added; otherwise reject."
- **mentor**: strongest: It fits the mentor almost exactly: it asks whether letting a Gödel-style loop edit its own improver pays off, and under what environmental pressure. The secondary prediction is the least scooped and most interesting part: improver-edit rate rises after stale-rule harm in the previous era. It gives a mechanistic, falsifiable account of why meta-harnesses rarely update themselves, and a null result (still no improver edits even when drift forces it) is a publishable capability-gap finding. A time-split, certified real-library drift benchmark would also be a reusable artifact the field lacks. · weakest: The stream design is taken from Are-SE-CL (drift typed by kind, compound switching streams, a regrouped mixed control), so reviewers will read this as "Are-SE-CL + an S4 arm on real SWE data" (partial K4). Drift type is also confounded with era order and difficulty. E2, the silent semantic change, is the hard era by nature, and E3 removal produces loud errors that memory (S2) alone can fix. So a Δ(c−b) could reflect error signal, not an "improver must change" mechanism. The scope is too large for a solo undergrad: about 8 libraries × 4 eras × 60 certified tasks, 5 arms × 2 streams × 5 lineages, plus the 2x2 with VERSE and the Harness^2 and MetaEvoForge baselines. With 20 held-out tasks per era per library, a 10 pp interaction is likely underpowered. The "law" (β against switch count) has at most 3 points and cannot be identified. · must fix: (1) Cut the scope to 3-4 libraries, 3 eras (shim, then silent change, then removal), arms (a), (b), (c) and (d) plus ICL, and drop the "law". (2) Separate drift type from era position. Either counterbalance era order across lineages (e.g. silent change before deprecation in some streams), or treat the regrouped control as the main comparison and do a power analysis on the arm×stream interaction before collecting data. (3) Cross S4 with verification availability (execution replay on or off) in a 2x2 to rule out VERSE's verification explanation. (4) Preregister a smaller effect (about 4-5 pp) and make the edit-rate-after-stale-harm prediction the headline hypothesis, with the accuracy gap secondary. (5) Have a human audit 10-15% of the certified tasks and report contamination checks against the model's cutoff for each library version. (6) Use matched tokens, and count improver-edit tokens against the arm's budget, so S4/S5 does not get free extra search.
  > ""The question of when improver self-modification pays is timely and well motivated, but the stream construction closely follows Are-SE-CL, and drift type is confounded with era order and error salience. Without counterbalanced orderings and a verification control, I cannot tell whether the S4 gains come from non-stationarity or from richer failure signals in later eras.""

### Synthetic Drift Lies About Harness Learners: Rank Disagreement and Overstated Forgetting Relative to Matched Real Histories (`reality-synthetic-vs-real-agreement`) — panel 61.8/100

**Claim.** For 6 continual harness-learning methods, ranks on synthetic drift streams agree poorly with ranks on real-history streams matched to them in change count and era length (Kendall τ < 0.4). Synthetic streams also overstate forgetting by at least 1.5×, and the overstatement grows with how abrupt the synthetic transitions are.

**If the opposite holds.** If ranks agree (τ > 0.7) and forgetting is matched, synthetic streams are validated as cheap proxies, a useful methodological result that licenses Δτ/AgentDriftBench-style benchmarks and saves the field from costly real mining. In either case the paired real/synthetic suite is released.

**Law.** Forgetting_synth / Forgetting_real ≈ 1 + γ · A, where A ∈ [0,1] is the abruptness of the synthetic transitions. The prediction is γ > 0, with γ near zero when the real stream is itself abrupt (removal releases). This is the harness-learner analogue of hip2PfZSRg.

**Design.** Real streams: 3 sources.
- API releases: reuse the DriftEras pipeline from the first idea.
- REST/MCP schema release pairs: ContractPatch-style, about 40 certified migrations.
- Web: BreakingWeb self-hosted apps replayed across real upstream release tags. These are the mentor's apps. The assumption to check is whether the upstream projects publish tagged releases that change the DOM.

Synthetic twins: for each real stream, generate a twin with the same number of changes per era and the same per-task change density, using Δτ-style LLM transforms (rename/split/merge) or AgentDriftBench-style injection. Each twin is produced at 3 abruptness levels: all changes at the era boundary; spread over half of the era; spread linearly.

Methods, all on the shared Gödel-lite testbed at a matched token budget:
- full-context ICL;
- a GEPA-style prompt evolver;
- an ACE-style playbook;
- a skill library with versioning;
- Meta-Harness-lite;
- Gödel-lite with S4/S5 editable.

Models: Qwen3-14B and 32B on vLLM. Five seeds per method × stream.

Metrics:
- final held-out accuracy;
- backward transfer / forgetting on dormant-return probes (Continual-ARC-style);
- plasticity–stability correlation.
The primary test is Kendall τ between real and synthetic rankings, with a permutation null and a bootstrap CI over seeds. The secondary test regresses the forgetting ratio on abruptness, with source as a random effect.
Horizons and segment definitions are preregistered, as 2609.31640 and 2610.41792 recommend.

**Pilot.** Use the API source only (2 libraries, 3 eras), 3 methods (ICL, GEPA-style, Gödel-lite), the synthetic twin at abrupt and gradual settings, and 3 seeds on Qwen3-14B. About $25 for drafting the twins.
Go if at least one pairwise rank flip between real and abrupt-synthetic appears across seeds with non-overlapping CIs, or if forgetting differs by at least 1.3×.
Kill if every metric matches within CI. In that case reframe as a validation note and fold the infrastructure into the first idea.

**Closest work (author's view).** Δτ-Bench 2610.34238 and AgentDriftBench 5xFWggnePr use synthetic drift only. Are-SE-CL 2610.25858 uses synthetic block streams. hip2PfZSRg ('Do NNs Lose Plasticity in a Gradually Changing World?') addresses weight-level plasticity, not harness learners. WebVariants TYSv9rIhiQ shows rank reversals across implementations, not across real versus synthetic drift. None measures agreement between real and synthetic drift for harness learners.

**Scoop check: open.** No scoop found. No 2026 paper or ICLR 2027 submission measures real-vs-synthetic drift agreement for harness learners, or tests whether synthetic drift inflates their forgetting. Fixes needed to survive review:
(1) Position against Are-SE-CL's blocked-vs-Mix appendix as the only existing schedule-shape evidence. Cite BenchPulse and WebVariants for rank fragility in frozen models, then make 'continual learners plus matched real twins' the explicit gap.
(2) Kendall tau over 6 methods is badly underpowered. The permutation null over 720 orderings cannot separate tau=0.4 from noise. Use per-stream and per-seed pairwise-preference agreement (15 method pairs x streams x seeds) or rank-biased overlap with a hierarchical bootstrap, and add 2-3 cheap methods (e.g. ReasoningBank, HCL-style guarded commit).
(3) Make the forgetting-ratio-vs-abruptness law the headline. It is more novel than 'ranks disagree' and works within each source. Include real abrupt removal releases as a built-in falsification check (gamma should be about 0 there).
(4) Check that BreakingWeb upstream tags actually change the DOM before committing. TimeWarp's 6-era UIs are a fallback, and real MCP/OpenAPI changelogs a backup.
(5) Match twins on edit-type distribution (rename/split/remove proportions), not only change count. Otherwise reviewers will say the gap comes from edit type, not from how synthetic the stream is.

| Paper | Title | Summary | Overlap |
|---|---|---|---|
| [2610.25858](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25858) | Are Self-Evolving Agents Continual Learners? (Mila/GDM, Oct 2026) | Runs ReasoningBank, GEPA and Meta-Harness on 4 synthetic block-drift streams built from BIRD, BFCL and ScienceWorld. Finds a plasticity-stability trade-off (r=-0.72) and forgetting through prompt overwriting. Appendix A compares blocked streams with a uniformly interleaved Mix stream; GEPA's terminal gain drops from +5.8 to +0.9. | Medium. It uses the same harness-learner CL metrics and already includes a crude abruptness contrast (blocked vs. interleaved), where the ordering of methods changes. It has no real version-history streams and no real-vs-synthetic rank agreement. Expect reviewers to cite its Mix result as partial prior evidence that schedule shape changes conclusions. |
| [hpoWWEDJW9](https://openreview.net/forum?id=hpoWWEDJW9) | BenchPulse: Monitoring Verdict and Ranking Drift in Evolving Agent Benchmarks (ICLR 2027 submission) | Censuses 4,119 real commits across 15 agent benchmarks. Shows that real benchmark updates (to verifiers, environments and aggregation) flip verdicts and reverse model orderings, e.g. 4 reversals on tau2-bench and 893 on BFCL. | Low to medium. It is real-version-history evidence of rank instability, but for frozen models under benchmark-contract changes, not for continual learners. It has no synthetic twins and no measure of forgetting. It weakens a 'rankings are fragile' framing on its own. |
| [3hwygrPlXH](https://openreview.net/forum?id=3hwygrPlXH) | When Does Continual Learning Require Learning (ICLR 2027 submission) | A unified update-operator framework comparing SFT, RL, prompt optimization, retrieval and context compression on Qwen3-8B. Uses sequential streams built from real benchmarks, including real factual-change and financial temporal drift. | Low to medium. It compares harness-style and weight-level methods on real temporal drift. It does not build synthetic twins and does not test rank agreement or forgetting inflation. |
| [2603.05910](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05910) | The World Won't Stay Still: ProEvolve (Amazon/Berkeley) | Synthetic, version-linked environment evolution through graph transformations: completion, saturation and deprecation. Covers 200 e-commerce versions. Memory strategies (trace or summary) cut tool calls but hurt success under change. | Low. It is a synthetic-drift generator only, with LLM-judged 'Reality' scores rather than any check against real releases. It could serve as one more synthetic generator family for the twins. |
| 5xFWggnePr / 2610.34238 | AgentDriftBench (ICLR27) and Delta-tau-Bench | Synthetic mid-evaluation schema drift (5 families) with recovery metrics; 6 successive synthetic ecosystem states produced by capability-preserving transforms. | Low. These are the synthetic generators the idea critiques. Neither one validates against real release histories. |
| [dVv1MBkavP](https://openreview.net/forum?id=dVv1MBkavP) | Harness Continual Learning: Continual Adaptation beyond Model Parameters (ICLR 2027 submission) | Formalizes harness-level forgetting and proposes guarded harness evolution (the update is committed only after a retention check). Evaluated on constructed capability-accumulation streams. | Low. It is a method and framing paper. It could be added as a 7th method or used to frame the problem. |
| [2603.04949](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04949) | TimeWarp: Evaluating Web Agents by Revisiting the Past | 3 web environments, each with 6 UI versions emulating different eras of web design. | Low, but useful. It offers a ready-made multi-version web substrate if BreakingWeb upstream tags turn out to change the DOM too little. Its versions are reconstructed, not literal upstream releases. |
| 2602.09234 (hip2PfZSRg) | Do Neural Networks Lose Plasticity in a Gradually Changing World? | Shows that loss of plasticity in weight-level learners is largely an artifact of abrupt task switches. | Conceptual ancestor, as the author says. It is the weight-level analogue of the abruptness law and does not cover harness learners. The 2608.04927 'Optimal Training-Time Scaling in Gradual Adaptation' paper is a related theory paper. |

**Judge panel**

| Lens | N | S | R | C | F | T | Total | Verdict |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ac | 3.5 | 3 | 2.5 | 2.5 | 2.5 | 4 | 59.6 | fold-into-another |
| expert | 3.5 | 3.5 | 3 | 3 | 2.5 | 4 | 65 | pursue-with-fixes |
| mentor | 3.5 | 3 | 3 | 3 | 2 | 3.5 | 60.8 | fold-into-another |

- **ac**: strongest: The gap is real and checkable. No 2026 paper or ICLR 2027 submission tests whether the synthetic drift generators (Delta-tau-Bench, AgentDriftBench, ProEvolve) give the same conclusions about continual harness learners as real release histories do. The forgetting-ratio vs abruptness law (gamma > 0, with gamma about 0 on real abrupt removal releases as a built-in falsification check) is a sharp, falsifiable, quantitative claim. It speaks to the user's question about a benchmark grounded in reality, and the paired real/synthetic suite is a reusable artifact whichever way the result goes. · weakest: The abruptness axis is confounded, and the direction of the prediction may be wrong. Real API, schema and web releases are step changes at release tags, so the 'all changes at the era boundary' twin is arguably the most realistic one, and a 'gradual' twin has no real counterpart. If so, gamma measures how far the twin departs from the real step shape, not how much synthetic streams overstate forgetting. The same problem hits the forgetting metric. Dormant-return probes need old versions to come back, and real histories rarely revert, so the real-side forgetting signal either barely exists or has to be built synthetically. That would make the comparison circular. On top of this, Kendall tau over 6 methods is badly underpowered. The cost is also large: 6 methods x 2 models x 5 seeds x 3 sources x 4 conditions (real plus 3 abruptness levels), on top of three separate real-mining pipelines, which a solo undergrad with $3k will struggle to deliver. The idea also depends on the DriftEras/ContractPatch/BreakingWeb infrastructure from other ideas. It is a methods-validation side study, only loosely tied to the user's main questions (how open the search space is vs how effective the system is, why meta-harnesses rarely update themselves). · must fix: (1) Define abruptness for the real streams themselves: measure each real stream's empirical A, using deprecation windows, staged rollouts and multi-release migrations. Then regress the forgetting ratio on the gap A_synth - A_real, not on A_synth alone. Include a twin that matches A_real as the null condition. (2) Define forgetting on the real side without synthetic reverts. Options are long-tail tasks that go dormant and then reappear in later real eras, or backward-compatibility probes on still-supported old endpoints. (3) Replace the 6-method Kendall tau with pairwise-preference agreement (15+ pairs x streams x seeds) under a hierarchical bootstrap, and add 2-3 cheap methods. (4) Match twins on edit-type mix (rename/split/remove/semantic) as well as change count. Add an ablation that matches edit type but changes only the generator, to separate 'synthetic' from 'edit-type' effects. (5) Cut scope: one model at full depth (Qwen3-14B), with 32B for a single replication; at most 2 real sources (API plus MCP/OpenAPI); TimeWarp as the web fallback. (6) Run it as the validation chapter of the DriftEras paper rather than as a standalone submission. A 'synthetic is a fine proxy' null result on its own reads as a workshop note.
  > ""The authors claim synthetic drift overstates forgetting as transitions become more abrupt. But the real streams they compare against are release-tag step functions, which are maximally abrupt. Without measuring the real streams' own abruptness, and without a forgetting signal on the real side that does not depend on synthetic reverts, I cannot tell whether gamma measures how synthetic the stream is or just a mismatch in schedule shape. Rank agreement over six methods with five seeds also lacks the power to support the tau < 0.4 headline. An interesting question with a reusable dataset, but in its current form it is a benchmark-validation appendix, not a NeurIPS main-track contribution.""
- **expert**: strongest: The gap is real and it lands at the right moment. Δτ-Bench, AgentDriftBench and ProEvolve are multiplying with no check against real history. A paired real/synthetic suite plus an abruptness-to-forgetting law with a falsification arm built in (γ≈0 on real abrupt removal releases) gives a mechanistic result that holds either way it comes out. It also answers the user's request for continual-learning tests 'grounded in reality'. Released twins would become infrastructure for later benchmarks. · weakest: 'Matched twin' is confounded by construction. However the twins are matched (count, density, even edit type), real changes still differ in semantic coherence, co-change structure, deprecation-then-removal sequences and docs-lag. Any rank or forgetting gap can then be put down to an unmatched nuisance property rather than to 'syntheticness'. On top of that, Kendall τ over 6 methods is too weak to tell 0.4 from noise. The full grid also exceeds what a solo undergrad can run in 6 months on ≤$3k: 6 methods × 3 sources × 4 stream variants × 5 seeds × 2 models ≈ 720 continual runs, plus mining 3 real sources, one of which (BreakingWeb upstream DOM tags) is unverified. · must fix: (1) Replace the single twin with a matching ladder: twin_0 matches count only, then edit-type mix is added, then co-change/dependency structure, then real natural-language changelog semantics. Report how much of the real-vs-synthetic gap each rung closes, so a confound becomes a decomposition result. (2) Make the γ-abruptness law the headline, with real abrupt removal releases as the preregistered γ≈0 control. Move rank agreement to secondary and measure it with pairwise-preference agreement (15 or more pairs × streams × seeds, hierarchical bootstrap), not 6-item Kendall τ. (3) Cut the scope: 2 sources (API + MCP/OpenAPI changelogs, with TimeWarp only if BreakingWeb tags fail a DOM-diff check in week 1), 4-5 methods including Gödel-lite and an ACE-style playbook, and 3 seeds on Qwen3-14B with a 32B or second-family model only on a subset. Budget GPU-hours explicitly. (4) Add at least one non-Qwen open model family so the law is not model-specific.
  > "The abruptness-forgetting law is the interesting contribution, and the removal-release control is a nice touch. But the authors never show that their 'synthetic' and 'real' streams differ only in syntheticness. Without an ablation over matched properties, I cannot tell whether the gap reflects synthetic generation or the particular rename/split/merge transform family they chose. The rank-agreement analysis over six methods is statistically uninformative as presented."
- **mentor**: strongest: The gap is real and checkable. No one has validated the synthetic drift generators (Delta-tau, AgentDriftBench, ProEvolve) against matched real release histories for continual harness learners. The paired real/synthetic suite would be a reusable artifact that the field's growing pile of synthetic-drift benchmarks would have to cite. The forgetting-ratio-vs-abruptness law has a built-in falsification arm: gamma should be about 0 on real removal releases. That makes it a sharper claim than "rankings are fragile." · weakest: The abruptness axis is conceptually confounded. Real version histories are already step changes at release boundaries, so the "gradual" synthetic twins are the less realistic ones. Any forgetting gap is then more likely driven by edit content and semantics (LLM rename/split vs. real deprecations), not by abruptness, and the law ends up measuring the wrong variable. On top of that, the scope is too wide for a solo undergrad in 6 months on a modest GPU: three real-stream pipelines (one depends on an unbuilt "first idea", and the BreakingWeb upstream tags are unverified), 6-8 methods, 2 models including 32B, 3 abruptness levels, and 5 seeds. With 6 methods, Kendall tau is statistically meaningless. And the "synthetic is fine" outcome is a validation note that would go to the Datasets and Benchmarks track, not NeurIPS main, so the "either outcome publishable" claim is only half true. · must fix: (1) Cut to one real source: API releases (DriftEras), or TimeWarp as the web substrate if BreakingWeb tags turn out not to change the DOM. Drop Qwen3-32B from the main grid. (2) Replace Kendall tau over 6 methods with pairwise-preference agreement (method pairs x streams x seeds) under a hierarchical bootstrap, and add 2-3 cheap methods. (3) Decompose the gap into factors: match the twins on edit-type distribution, and run a 2x2 of real-vs-LLM edit content against real-vs-synthetic timing. That way abruptness is separated from content, and the law tests the variable it names. (4) Make the forgetting-inflation law the headline, and position it explicitly against the blocked-vs-Mix result in Are-SE-CL (2610.25858) and against BenchPulse. (5) Better still, run this as the validation section inside the DriftEras benchmark paper rather than as a standalone paper.
  > ""The authors show that LLM-generated drift differs from real release drift, but real releases are themselves abrupt. The 'abruptness' manipulation therefore changes content and timing at the same time, and the proposed law cannot tell them apart. With six methods, the rank-agreement statistic has essentially no power. This reads as a careful benchmark-validation appendix rather than a main-track contribution.""
